This review discusses what is known about the FA proteins and other recently linked FA-like proteins. The goal is to clarify how the proteins work together to carry out interstrand crosslink repair and homologous recombination-mediated repair of damaged DNA.

=== idx: 283 ===
Gold supporting: It has been previously shown that disruption of RAD26 in yeast strain W303-1B results in a strain that is deficient in transcription-coupled repair (TCR), the preferential repair of the transcribed strand of an expressed gene over the non-transcribed strand and the rest of the genome.
LLM supporting:  The provided context does not explicitly mention the specific gene strand targeted by transcription-coupled repair (TCR). However, based on the general understanding of TCR, it primarily targets the non-template (non-coding) strand of DNA. This is because TCR is a mechanism that specifically recognizes and corrects errors in the DNA template strand, which is transcribed into mRNA. The non-template strand, also known as the coding strand, is not directly involved in the transcription process but is used as a reference for the correct sequence of the transcribed mRNA. Therefore, when a mutation or error occurs in the DNA template strand, TCR can identify and correct it, ensuring that the resulting mRNA is accurate. This process is crucial for maintaining the integrity of gene expression and preventing the propagation of errors in the genetic code.

ROUGE-1: 0.3243 ROUGE-2: 0.0765 ROUGE-L: 0.1946

The elevated TSH levels can lead to further damage to the thyroid gland if left untreated. LLM supporting:  The formation of antiparallel heterodimers by alpha-spectrin and beta-spectrin subunits is supported by the following observations from the context: (1) In erythroid spectrin (αIβI), the first two paired repeats (βIR1 with αIR21, and βIR2 with αRI20) at one end of the dimer are necessary and sufficient to unite the chains. (2) The strength of the interaction is significantly increased when the adjoining pair of repeats (βIR3-αIR19) is added. (3) In brain spectrin (αIIβII), the first two pairs of repeats are similarly essential and sufficient for heterodimer formation. (4) The interaction in brain spectrin is approximately 60-fold stronger than in the erythroid counterpart, but no further enhancement is detected with additional repeats. (5) A tight αIβI dimer likely depends on the structural coupling of the first two repeats in each chain. (6) Sequence analysis of the strongly interacting repeats (βIR1, βIIR1, αIR21, and αIIR20) and their homologs in α-actinin provides insight into the evolutionary divergence and explains the different properties of the two spectrin isoforms in terms of inter-chain interaction stability. These findings collectively indicate that the dimerization of alpha-spectrin and beta-spectrin subunits occurs in an antiparallel manner.

No correlations were found between age and uptake in the cerebellum, the regional cerebral blood flow, or the time course of metabolization of [18F]altanserin. The reduction in cerebral 5-HT2A receptor binding thus directly reflects the loss of specific 5-HT2A receptors with age.

To determine the number of genera in the Flaviviridae family, we need to consider the established taxonomic classification. The Flaviviridae family is indeed divided into four genera: Flavivirus, Hepatovirus, Cardiovirus, and Furovirus. This classification is based on the International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses (ICTV) report. Each genus includes viruses that share common characteristics in terms of their genome structure, replication strategy, and host range. For instance, the Flavivirus genus includes well-known viruses such as dengue virus, Zika virus, and West Nile virus, while the Hepatovirus genus includes hepatitis C virus (HCV) and hepatitis G virus (HGV). The Cardiovirus genus includes viruses like EMC and D614, and the Furovirus genus includes furovirus, which is primarily found in plants.

LLM supporting:  Phospholamban interactome in cardiac contractility and survival: A new vision of an old friend. Depressed sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) calcium cycling, reflecting impaired SR Ca-transport and Ca-release, is a key and universal characteristic of human and experimental heart failure. These SR processes are regulated by multimeric protein complexes, including protein kinases and phosphatases as well as their anchoring and regulatory subunits that fine-tune Ca-handling in specific SR sub-compartments. Recent studies identified additional regulatory proteins, associated with PLN, that control SR Ca-transport. These include the inhibitor-1 (I-1) of protein phosphatase 1 (PP1), the small heat shock protein 20 (Hsp20) and the HS-1 associated protein X-1 (HAX1). In addition, the intra-luminal histidine-rich calcium binding protein (HRC) has been shown to interact with both SERCA2a and triadin. Notably, there is physical and direct interaction between these protein players, mediating a fine-cross talk between SR Ca-uptake, storage and release. Importantly, regulation of SR Ca-cycling by the PLN/SERCA interactome does not only impact cardiomyocyte contractility, but also survival and remodeling. Indeed, naturally occurring variants in these Ca-cycling genes modulate their activity and interactions with other protein partners, resulting in depressed contractility and accelerated remodeling. These genetic variants may serve as potential prognostic or diagnostic markers in cardiac pathophysiology.

Importantly, regulation of SR Ca-cycling by the PLN/SERCA interactome does not only impact cardiomyocyte contractility, but also survival and remodeling. Indeed, naturally occurring variants in these Ca-cycling genes modulate their activity and interactions with other protein partners, resulting in depressed contractility and accelerated remodeling. Depressed sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) calcium cycling, reflecting impaired SR Ca-transport and Ca-release, is a key and universal characteristic of human and experimental heart failure. These SR processes are regulated by multimeric protein complexes, including protein kinases and phosphatases as well as their anchoring and regulatory subunits that fine-tune Ca-handling in specific SR sub-compartments. Recent studies identified additional regulatory proteins, associated with PLN, that control SR Ca-transport. Importantly, regulation of SR Ca-cycling by the PLN/SERCA interactome does not only impact cardiomyocyte contractility, but also survival and remodeling. Indeed, naturally occurring variants in these Ca-cycling genes modulate their activity and interactions with other protein partners, resulting in depressed contractility and accelerated remodeling.
